Edwards Aquifer, San Antonio TX segment — first EPA sole-source aquifer designation (40 FR 58344) (1975)Landmark Precedent
SDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionSDWA §1424(e) designation — federal-funding review gate (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

The sleeping federal hook for aquifer-country siting: it reaches projects only through federal money, but any federally backed loan, grant, or infrastructure assistance touching a data-center build-out over a designated aquifer (the Edwards region overlaps the Texas data-center boom; the Avra Basin designation sits under Tucson's) opens an EPA contamination review with funding-denial teeth.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Not an enforcement action: in December 1975 EPA designated the Edwards Aquifer — the sole drinking-water source for San Antonio — as the nation's first sole-source aquifer. Designation triggers EPA review of any federally financially-assisted project over the aquifer's recharge zone; EPA can deny federal funds to projects that may contaminate the aquifer and create a significant public-health hazard.

Outcome

The program became a standing federal review gate: in 1991 alone EPA reviewed 152 federal-assistance projects worth $571M over sole-source aquifers nationwide, requiring modification of 25. The Edwards designation also carries unique aquifer-specific federal regulations (40 C.F.R. Part 149 Subpart A).

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Sole-source aquifer project review
Complementary rather than alternative to the CWA: §1424(e) reviews the funding of a project over an aquifer, while §404/§402 reach its construction and discharges. Federally financed AI-infrastructure programs would bring this reading into play for aquifer-sited campuses.
Historic examples in this record: Rowan ProjectCinco Medina TX (2025) · ProjectBlue Tucson AMES (2026)
SDWA Section 1424(e) (42 U.S.C. 300h-3(e)) — sole-source aquifer designation and federal project review (40 C.F.R. Part 149)
Sources

United States v. Republic Steel Corp., 362 U.S. 482 (1960) — industrial waste solids shoaling the Calumet River (with International Harvester and Interlake Iron)Landmark Precedent
RHAIndustrial discharge (§402)No CWA actionRHA §§10/13 — SCOTUS injunction against industrial deposits (pre-CWA)
Relevance to data centers

Two lessons for the data-center era: courts will read old water statutes expansively against new industrial fact patterns, and Corps jurisdiction over structures and obstructions in navigable waters (Section 10) — untouched by Sackett — independently reaches cooling intakes, outfall structures, and river crossings that hyperscale campuses and their power plants are now proposing.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Three South Chicago steel mills discharged industrial waste solids through sewers into the Calumet River, where the suspended solids settled and shoaled the navigable channel. The United States sued to enjoin the discharges and compel dredging.

Outcome

The Supreme Court held the deposits were an 'obstruction' under Section 10 and prohibited discharges under Section 13 — the 'liquid state' sewage exemption does not cover industrial solids in suspension — and authorized injunctive relief including dredging at the companies' expense. The decision revived the 1899 Refuse Act as a water-pollution enforcement tool a decade before the CWA existed.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: RHA — Structures and obstructions in navigable waters
The CWA §402 permit program replaced Refuse Act prosecutions for discharges, but RHA §10 survives independently: any data-center intake or outfall structure in a traditionally navigable water still needs Corps §10 authorization on top of NPDES — a Sackett-proof second permit gate.
Historic examples in this record: AWS LakeAnnaVA VPDES cooling discharge (2026)
Rivers and Harbors Act of 1899, Sections 10 and 13 (33 U.S.C. 403, 407) — obstruction of navigable capacity; the Refuse Act
Sources
United States v. Republic Steel Corp., 362 U.S. 482 (1960) (Justia) (court) · Cornell LII opinion text (law)

Part 3 — Active & Potential CWA Exposure at Named Data Center Sites (14)

14 named data center sites where regulatory proceedings are active (pending permit applications, ongoing investigations, active citizen suits) or where the factual circumstances match the historical enforcement patterns above — but no formal CWA enforcement action has been issued yet. Use the theories panel above to trace which CWA hook applies to each site.

Meta Platforms Inc., Stanton Springs / Newton-Morgan County, Georgia data center campus (2026)Data Center
SDWAGroundwater & aquifersCWA potentialEPA investigation pledged — no enforcement action yet
Relevance to data centers

Highest-profile potential CWA enforcement matter against a hyperscaler currently active, but at this point it is a pledged federal investigation, not an enforcement action. Distinguishing this from a closed case matters: as of late May 2026, no hyperscaler has been formally cited under CWA in this matter. The case illustrates both the regulatory attention now focused on data centers and the lag between resident complaints and formal agency action.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Residents of Morgan County, Georgia adjacent to Meta's Stanton Springs data center report well water turning brown and turbid following blasting and clearing for data center construction; appliances failing; families reliant on bottled water. Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez presented jars of discolored water at a congressional hearing in May 2026. EPA Assistant Administrator for Water Jessica Kramer publicly committed at that hearing to review whether the construction project has affected drinking water quality.

Outcome

PENDING — no formal CWA enforcement action, NOV, or consent order issued as of 2026-05-26. EPA review announced but not concluded. Meta commissioned its own independent groundwater study claiming no operational impact. No Georgia EPD enforcement action publicly documented.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Emergency powers — imminent endangerment of drinking water · SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
If construction runoff or blasting sediment reaches streams or wetlands, the §402 construction general permit applies — the Stillwater and Boydton template. If contaminants are migrating to wells via groundwater that ultimately reaches surface water, Maui's 'functional equivalent' test could require an NPDES permit; the wells themselves are Safe Drinking Water Act territory, outside the CWA.
Historic examples in this record: Google Stillwater OK stormwater (2025) · Microsoft Boydton VA (2023) · County of Maui v Hawaii Wildlife Fund (2020)
Not yet formally cited. Potential CWA Section 402 (construction stormwater) and Safe Drinking Water Act review; EPA Office of Water has only pledged investigation, not issued an enforcement action.
Sources
EPA Official Agrees to Review Data Center Impacts on Water (news) · AOC presses EPA over Morgan County drinking water concerns tied to Georgia data center development (news) · Meta data center allegedly muddies Georgia town's drinking water, investigation underway (news)
Nscale / Fidelis New Energy — Monarch Compute Campus, University Lane, Mason County, West Virginia (Meadowlands Estates adjacent) (2026)Data Center
CWAConstruction stormwaterCWA potentialPotential §402 construction stormwater — WV DEP
Relevance to data centers

The most recent and most visible 'construction stormwater fails catastrophically' incident in the data center sector. Even where the site was 'permit-compliant' the week before, AI-era buildout pace plus climate-intensified rainfall produced real-world property damage — exactly the failure mode that hardens citizen-suit pipelines and fuels stricter state CGP terms.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

On May 23-24, 2026, severe flooding hit Meadowlands Estates after a silt-fence section in the construction stormwater retention area at the Monarch/Nscale data center site failed during a rain event delivering roughly a month's rainfall in 48 hours under a Flash Flood Warning. Nscale acknowledged the storm 'exceeded the design capacity' of the temporary erosion controls. Civil reimbursement, not federal enforcement, is the primary current track.

Outcome

PENDING as of May 26, 2026 — No formal CWA NOV or consent order issued. Nscale committed to cover all cleanup/repair costs, hotel rooms, and meals for affected residents. West Virginia National Guard and state officials were on-site coordinating evaluation; WV DEP discussing corrective and prevention plans. No filed civil suit or class action yet reported.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: CWA — Construction stormwater (CGP)
A silt-fence/retention failure that discharges sediment off-site is the classic §402 construction-stormwater violation — WV DEP can issue an NOV under its delegated construction general permit, and downstream residents could serve a §505 citizen-suit notice.
Historic examples in this record: Microsoft Boydton VA (2023) · Google Stillwater OK stormwater (2025) · Energix VA (2024)
Potential CWA Section 402 (NPDES construction stormwater) — formal enforcement under West Virginia DEP authority pending. As of May 25, 2026 no NOV had been issued; a WV DEP inspection the week prior had found the site in compliance.
Sources
Severe flooding in Mason County relating to data center construction — WSAZ (news) · Some Mason County residents experience flooding near site of Nscale development — Charleston Gazette-Mail (news) · Mason County community impacted by flooding from data center construction site — WV MetroNews (news)
AMES Construction (general contractor for Beale Infrastructure / Project Blue hyperscale data center, South Houghton Road, Pima County, Arizona — believed to be an Amazon Web Services site, though Beale has not confirmed end-user) (2026)Data Center
SDWAWater supply & drinking waterNo CWA actionMunicipal water denial + CAA dust NOV (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

Highlights the convergence of CWA-adjacent stormwater/dust control, municipal water-permit politics, and groundwater extraction permitting at a single hyperscale site. Tucson is the first major U.S. city to formally deny municipal water service to a data center on policy grounds — a precedent that may shift hyperscale projects toward private groundwater wells subject to state withdrawal permits rather than CWA-regulated discharges.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

On May 8 and May 11, 2026, Pima County DEQ inspectors documented excess fugitive dust crossing property boundary lines from the Project Blue construction site, with no dust-control measures in use. Pima County issued a Notice of Violation to AMES Construction on or about May 13, 2026, with potential fines of up to $10,000 per day per violation under Arizona state law. Root cause: the City of Tucson's May 2026 revocation of AMES's water-meter hydrant permit (after the city declared Project Blue ineligible for any municipal resources, including water), forcing the contractor to halt dust-suppression water trucking before establishing an alternative non-potable source. Separately, ADWR approved well-drilling permits for Bobcat Tucson Water LLC (a Beale Infrastructure SPV) for two on-site wells authorized to withdraw up to 96.5 acre-feet (~31.4 million gallons) per year.

Outcome

PENDING — AMES had until May 17, 2026 to respond to the NOV. Beale Infrastructure paused site work while the contractor formalized a non-potable water source. No penalty finalized as of late May 2026. City of Tucson has refused to provide any water service for the project; the project's permanent water supply will rely on on-site groundwater wells under ADWR permits.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
The CWA has no handle on a municipal water-service denial. The construction site itself still carries §402 stormwater obligations — sediment or dust-suppression runoff leaving the site would be enforceable the way Stillwater and Boydton were.
Historic examples in this record: Google Stillwater OK stormwater (2025) · Microsoft Boydton VA (2023)
Pima County DEQ fugitive-dust permit (Arizona implementation of CAA), tied directly to the project's water-supply dispute — City of Tucson revoked AMES's hydrant meter permit in May 2026, triggering construction water-use changes that caused the dust violation. Not a direct CWA NPDES action, but illustrates the water-permit / CWA-stormwater crossover at hyperscale sites.
Sources
Contractor working on Project Blue data center issued Notice of Violation (news) · Pima County cites Project Blue contractor for air quality violation — Tucson.com (news) · City says Project Blue must stop using Tucson Water for data center construction — Tucson Sentinel (news) · Project Blue's 2 new Tucson wells could draw up to 31 million gallons of water annually — Tucson Sentinel (news) · Project Blue FAQ — Pima County, AZ (state agency action)
Google LLC (applicant via 'Project Raspberry'), Greenfield Industrial Park, Daleville, Botetourt County, Virginia — three 300,000 sq ft data center buildings on 343.6 acres (2025-2026)Data Center
CWAWetlands & streams (§404/§401)CWA applied§404 individual permit application (in federal review)
Relevance to data centers

One of the largest §404 individual permit applications for a single data center campus currently in the federal pipeline in Virginia. The FOIA fight over water-use projections (8 MGD/day at full buildout) is itself precedent-setting: Virginia courts confirmed that data center water-use records held by public utilities are public records, not proprietary business information — a significant transparency ruling for the tracker's data-collection strategy.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Not an enforcement action — a §404 individual permit application in active federal review. Google's Project Raspberry data center campus in Botetourt County would impact approximately 3.24 acres of non-tidal wetlands and 6,715 linear feet of stream channels in unnamed tributaries to Tinker Creek and Rulman Branch (Roanoke River basin). Public notice issued March 10, 2026; comment period closed April 9, 2026. Separately, Google litigated through December 2025 to suppress its projected water use under Virginia FOIA; a judge ordered disclosure on November 5, 2025 — the disclosed figure was 8 million gallons per day at full campus buildout.

Outcome

PENDING — Army Corps environmental review ongoing as of June 2026. Virginia §401 WQC not yet issued. Google has offered to purchase wetland mitigation bank credits. The FOIA disclosure of 8 MGD projected water use drew public attention to the scale of data center water demand in the Roanoke Valley water shed.

Statute applicability: CWA — Dredge-and-fill of wetlands and streams

The cumulative wetland/stream impact of Virginia's data center build-out is now large enough to warrant cumulative-impact analysis under NEPA.

Relevance to data centers

The largest single §404 wetland impact in any active data center permit application, illustrating that hyperscale campuses can generate wetland and stream impacts an order of magnitude larger than construction stormwater GPs alone address. The 16.8-acre/6,000-foot footprint is a credible trigger for individual permit review rather than nationwide permits, giving the Corps and state §401 certifying authority substantial leverage over project design.

Google LLC (client) / Panattoni Development Co. (applicant/developer), “Project Cannoli” 1-GW data center campus, Van Buren Township, Wayne County, Michigan (Haggerty Road / I-94, Huron River watershed) (2026)Data Center
CWAWetlands & streams (§404/§401)CWA potential§404 individual permit + EGLE state wetland permit — under review, comment period closed 6/26/2026
Relevance to data centers

A textbook post-Sackett individual §404 permit fight at a hyperscaler campus — paired federal/state wetland review, a 13+ acre permanent wetland loss, and a multi-million-gallon-per-day municipal water draw — that gives commenters and potential litigants two separate permitting choke points (Corps §404 and EGLE state wetland permit) before a shovel goes in the ground.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Panattoni applied for federal §404 and state wetland permits to permanently fill 13.55 acres of wetlands and install six culverts in regulated streams, filling and abandoning 573 linear feet of stream channel, for a 282-acre, 1-GW data center campus. The Army Corps' public notice put total impacts at nearly 17 acres of wetlands and over 6,000 linear feet of streams when counting temporary impacts. The project is projected to use 2-3.6 million gallons of water per day, sourced from Van Buren Township's Great Lakes Water Authority municipal connection. EGLE's public comment period closed June 26, 2026; the Corps and U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service are conducting parallel federal review.

Outcome

PENDING — EGLE and USACE permit applications under review; no decision issued as of July 2026. Developer proposes 20.6 acres of mitigation bank credits in the Huron River watershed plus 1,174 feet of new on-site stream channel as compensatory mitigation.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: CWA — Dredge-and-fill of wetlands and streams · CWA — State water-quality certification
Corps §404 individual-permit review is already underway; EPA §404(c) veto or a §401 state-certification challenge remain live levers if the permitted mitigation is later found inadequate.
Historic examples in this record: Google ProjectRaspberry VA (2026) · Amazon NewCarlisle IN wetlands (2025) · Google FortWayneIN isolated wetland permit (2025)
§404 / 33 U.S.C. §1344 (Army Corps of Engineers) with a companion state wetland permit under Michigan's EGLE-administered program (Part 303, NREPA)
Sources
Google data center in Van Buren Township would destroy 13 acres of wetlands (Planet Detroit) (news) · Google data center in Van Buren Township would destroy 13 acres of wetlands (Michigan Advance) (news) · EGLE: Google data center pitched as community asset will destroy 13.5 acres of wetlands, use 3.6 million gallons of water per day (news)
xAI Corp. / CTC Property LLC — Colossus supercomputer campus and on-site greywater recycling plant, 3231 Paul R. Lowry Road, Memphis (Shelby County), Tennessee (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
CWASDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionState operating permit; no CWA discharge action
Relevance to data centers

The most prominent AI-data-center water fight to date shows the binding regulatory levers are usually NOT a CWA discharge violation: xAI's promised wastewater-reuse plant — which would have cut aquifer draw by up to 13 MGD — was permitted and then shelved, and no CWA mechanism compels it to build. This mirrors the tracker's core thesis: when a data center's operational water sits behind aquifer withdrawals and municipal-sewer arrangements, CWA discharge enforcement has little grip, and the real pressure points are state withdrawal permits, voluntary commitments, and (as the parallel air-permit suit shows) the Clean Air Act.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

xAI's Colossus campus draws cooling water from the Memphis Sand Aquifer, the city's drinking-water source. To offset that draw, xAI committed to an ~$80 million greywater recycling plant (permittee CTC Property LLC) that would treat effluent from the city's T.E. Maxson wastewater treatment plant for industrial cooling, preserving up to 13 million gallons per day of aquifer water. TDEC's Division of Water Resources accepted the application in February 2025 and held public hearings June 25–26, 2025; residents of the predominantly Black 38109 ZIP code raised environmental-justice objections about cumulative pollution burden. On April 9, 2026, xAI paused construction of the recycling plant, saying it was 'prioritizing other more immediate projects' (finishing the Colossus 2 supercomputer first) — leaving cooling demand on the aquifer in the interim.

Outcome

No CWA enforcement action, NOV, or penalty over water as of mid-2026. The greywater plant is permitted but indefinitely paused (announced April 9, 2026); aquifer-protection group Protect Our Aquifer called the pause 'deeply concerning' and 'alarming,' noting the plant 'was not optional — it was a core commitment.' Memphis Mayor Paul Young said he would keep pressing xAI to build it. Separately, the federal Clean Air Act citizen suit over the gas turbines remains active.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: CWA — NPDES permit for direct discharge · CWA — Pretreatment — industrial users of sewers · SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
Aquifer withdrawal volume is outside the CWA. But if the greywater plant (or the campus) ever discharges treated effluent to surface water it needs a §402 NPDES permit, and pollutants reaching the Mississippi via the aquifer could trigger Maui's functional-equivalent test.
Historic examples in this record: County of Maui v Hawaii Wildlife Fund (2020) · Google Berkeley SC Middendorf aquifer (2019)
Tennessee state water program — TDEC Division of Water Resources operating permit for the Colossus greywater recycling plant (CWA Section 402 NPDES is state-administered in Tennessee). Important framing: the active federal citizen suit against xAI (NAACP / Earthjustice, notice of intent filed Feb 2026) is a Clean AIR Act case over ~27 unpermitted methane gas turbines — NOT a CWA action. The water dimension here is the permitted-but-paused wastewater-reuse plant and the aquifer-withdrawal governance around it, not a discharge-violation enforcement order.
Sources
State Issues Operating Permit for Colossus Water Plant — Memphis Flyer (news) · 'Alarming:' xAI is 'prioritizing other projects'; Water plant is still planned — Action News 5 (news) · In South Memphis, Elon Musk's Colossus Operated Gas Turbines Without Appropriate Permits — Inside Climate News (news) · Illegal Pollution from Data Center Power Plants Shouldn't Harm Our Communities. We're Suing xAI. — Earthjustice (advocacy / litigation)
Atlas Development, LLC ('Project Sail', ~830-acre, ~$17B hyperscale campus, Coweta County, Georgia; end-user operator undisclosed); Coweta County government; ~17 resident petitioners. (2026)Data-Center Adjacent
SDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionState-court rezoning challenge (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

A model 'adjacent' case where the only live legal lever over a data center's water risk is local zoning plus a state groundwater-recharge-area designation — the CWA offers no handle on siting a campus over a drinking-water aquifer, so residents must litigate the rezoning itself to reach the water question.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

On May 7, 2026, seventeen residents filed suit asking a judge to void the county's rezoning and permanently block the campus, arguing the approval understated water and power needs, omitted wetlands, and ignored that the parcel sits atop a designated major groundwater-recharge area. A peer-reviewed study cited in the coverage projects a ~$118M decline in residential property values across ~1,215 parcels within 1.25 miles of the site.

Outcome

Pending in Coweta County Superior Court (appeal filed May 7, 2026, within 30 days of the rezoning decision).

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
If the parcel's omitted wetlands are jurisdictional (post-Sackett: a continuous surface connection to relatively permanent waters), grading them would need a §404 permit — the lever used at Amazon New Carlisle. Siting over a recharge area, by itself, is beyond the CWA.
Historic examples in this record: Sackett v EPA (2023) · Amazon NewCarlisle IN wetlands (2025)
No CWA action — a state-court rezoning challenge (declaratory-judgment / certiorari appeal) in Coweta County Superior Court under Georgia land-use law. Plaintiffs invoke the site's status as a Georgia DNR-designated 'Most Significant Groundwater Recharge Area' in the Middle Chattahoochee River basin and allege undisclosed wetlands.
Sources
Coweta County residents file appeal to stop massive data center on protected rural land (news) · Residents sue county government, developer over $17B data center construction (news) · Residents sue over $17 billion Georgia data center slated for major groundwater recharge area (news)
Quality Technology Services (QTS Realty Trust) data-center construction site, Fayette County, Georgia; Fayette County Water System. (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
SDWAWater supply & drinking waterNo CWA actionUtility metering / billing dispute (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

Shows data-center water accountability failing at the municipal-metering layer: millions of gallons can vanish from billing with no environmental statute, CWA permit, or penalty ever in play — a transparency gap distinct from pollution enforcement but central to the project's mission of tracking real data-center water use.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Roughly 13–15 million gallons of water used at the QTS site went unbilled over about six months while nearby Annelise Park residents reported low pressure during a Georgia drought. The county issued an ~$147,000 retroactive bill (a single six-month meter read accounting for ~$100,000) after discovering the meter was not being read electronically as assumed. County officials reported finding no evidence of widespread pressure problems after installing monitoring equipment.

Outcome

Resolved on billing — QTS paid the retroactive charges; no fine and no environmental enforcement. The county now meets regularly with QTS to monitor projected demand through the multi-year build.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
None plausible — metering and billing are utility-contract matters with no discharge, so no CWA section reaches them. The nearest water-accountability analog in this record is the same operator's construction-stormwater fight at Fayetteville.
Historic examples in this record: QTS Fayetteville GA (2024)
No CWA action — a county water-utility metering and billing dispute. One construction meter went un-captured during the county's switch to Advanced Metering Infrastructure (AMI); the county explicitly declined to fine QTS, characterizing it as a missed read, not unauthorized use.

TransGas Development Systems (New York-based); Adams Fork Energy Project — natural-gas-fueled ammonia plant and two data center facilities at Twisted Gun Golf Course (Wharncliffe) and Harless Industrial Park (Holden), Mingo County, West Virginia (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
CWAWetlands & streams (§404/§401)CWA applied§404 allegation via §505 citizen suit (pending)
Relevance to data centers

The first documented CWA §404 citizen suit targeting unpermitted stream/wetland disturbance at a data-center power-supply project. The framing — plaintiffs suing both the developer AND the Army Corps for regulatory inaction — is a template for future citizen enforcement where the Corps fails to require permits for data center construction in sensitive watersheds. The TRO denial does not preclude full merits review or injunctive relief after the administrative record is developed.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

On December 3, 2025, ten Mingo County residents filed a federal citizen suit in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of West Virginia, alleging TransGas has begun advancing ground-disturbing activities for the Adams Fork Energy Project — which would power two data center campuses — without obtaining required CWA §404 dredge-and-fill permits, ESA consultation, NEPA review, or FERC gas-pipeline approval. Plaintiffs named the Army Corps Huntington District, U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service, and FERC as defendants for failing to initiate required regulatory reviews of a project affecting streams and wetlands in the Tug Fork watershed.

Outcome

Judge denied plaintiffs' motion for a temporary restraining order and preliminary injunction. Litigation continuing as of June 2026. No Army Corps enforcement action, NOV, or permit denial has been independently issued.

Statute applicability: CWA — Dredge-and-fill of wetlands and streams · CWA — Citizen suits
Section 404 (alleged lack of Army Corps dredge-and-fill permit for project requiring stream/wetland disturbance in West Virginia). ESA Section 7 consultation, NEPA review, and NGA pipeline certificate also cited. No active agency enforcement order has been issued — this is a citizen suit alleging §404 violations; the Army Corps has not independently issued a NOV, cease-and-desist, or penalty.
Sources
Mingo County residents file federal lawsuit against company building power plants, data center in WV (news) · West Virginia residents file lawsuit against ammonia plant / data centre project (QC Intel) (news)
Ryan Companies US, Inc. (developer) and City of Pine Island, Minnesota (“Project Skyway,” 482-acre data center campus with Google as anchor tenant, Goodhue County) (2025-2026)Data-Center Adjacent
CWASDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionNo CWA instrument — state MEPA/AUAR review challenge + MGDPA records claim
Relevance to data centers

The clearest example yet of a state environmental-review statute (not the CWA) being used to force water-impact analysis before a data center campus breaks ground — plaintiffs specifically fault the environmental study for omitting groundwater/aquifer/private-well effects of a hyperscale campus's water demand, a fact pattern directly analogous to the CWA/SDWA gaps this tracker already watches in Georgia and Texas.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

The Minnesota Center for Environmental Advocacy sued the City of Pine Island and Ryan Companies in Goodhue County District Court (No. 25-CV-25-2298), arguing the city's Alternative Urban Areawide Review (AUAR) for the up-to-2,700-MW Project Skyway data center campus was legally inadequate because it failed to analyze how a water-use spike — developer filings put daily use at up to 152,000 gallons at buildout, while MCEA cites figures implying up to 55.7 million gallons annually (roughly 50% of the city's total current water use) — would affect groundwater, local aquifers, or nearby private wells, and used non-binding mitigation language instead of enforceable commitments. On May 22, 2026, the court granted a temporary restraining order blocking Ryan Companies from any construction or pre-construction activity, finding the City had not complied with the MGDPA in responding to MCEA's records requests.

Outcome

PENDING — TRO in effect as of the order date; litigation on the merits of the environmental review continues. Construction, originally slated to start July 2026, is delayed with the developer's own worst-case estimate slipping to 2027.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection · CWA — ‘Functional equivalent’ discharge through groundwater
If the campus's water draw is later shown to reach connected surface waters via shallow aquifer discharge, Maui's 'functional equivalent' theory could convert this into a federal CWA groundwater case; absent that, SDWA source-water-protection mapping is the likelier federal hook.
Historic examples in this record: CorpusChristi SintonTX EvangelineAquifer wells (2026) · Meta NewtonCountyGA well failures (2018-2025) · Sailfish HoodCountyTX ComancheCircle aquifer moratorium (2025-2026)
Not a CWA action — Minnesota Environmental Policy Act (MEPA) environmental-review adequacy challenge (Alternative Urban Areawide Review) plus a Minnesota Government Data Practices Act records claim
Sources
Court grants restraining order to halt Project Skyway progress in Pine Island (Post Bulletin) (news) · Lawsuit challenges Pine Island's scrutiny of data center (MPR News) (news) · Order Granting Motion for Temporary Restraining Order, Goodhue County District Court No. 25-CV-25-2298 (2026-05-22) (court ruling)
Rowan Digital Infrastructure ('Project Cinco', 440-acre data-center campus, Medina County, Texas; end-user undisclosed); Edwards Aquifer Authority (EAA); local suppliers including Yancey Water Supply. (2025)Data-Center Adjacent
SDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionTexas groundwater law / voluntary offsets (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

Captures the Texas pattern where the Rule of Capture and a state aquifer authority — not the CWA — govern a data center's water, and mitigation takes the form of voluntary offsets rather than enforceable discharge or withdrawal limits, leaving the public little regulatory leverage over consumption.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

A 440-acre data-center campus proposed to draw on the heavily stressed Edwards Aquifer during exceptional drought. Rather than a binding permit denial, the response has been negotiated and voluntary: a local water supplier demanded collaborative design and Rowan agreed to fund aquifer-conservation measures (helping shift farmers off flood irrigation), which the EAA framed as 'a reallocation of the existing water rights to these new purposes.' Multiple data centers are reportedly in development in the county.

Outcome

No permit denial; voluntary conservation investment and water-supply negotiation ongoing as of late 2025.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection · SDWA — Sole-source aquifer project review
Groundwater pumping under the Rule of Capture is invisible to the CWA. Discharge-side hooks appear only if the campus obtains a TPDES permit for blowdown and then exceeds it — the SpaceX and Hailiang TCEQ actions show what Texas enforcement looks like.
Historic examples in this record: SpaceX Starbase TX (2024) · Hailiang Copper TX (2023) · Google Berkeley SC Middendorf aquifer (2019)
No CWA action — Texas groundwater law: the Edwards Aquifer Authority's statutory pumping cap layered over Texas's common-law 'Rule of Capture,' plus a voluntary water-conservation arrangement. No NPDES discharge permit is involved.
Sources
The impact of billion-dollar data centers in water-stressed Medina County (news) · How One Texas County Struck a Deal With Its Data Centers (news) · Rowan Digital to invest in water conservation program in Medina County, Texas (trade press)
Google (Berkeley County data-center campus near Moncks Corner, South Carolina); South Carolina DHEC; Southern Environmental Law Center / Coastal Conservation League. (2016-2019)Data-Center Adjacent
SDWAGroundwater & aquifersNo CWA actionState withdrawal permit + private settlement (no CWA)
Relevance to data centers

A flagship example of a negotiated, voluntary-leaning groundwater commitment governed entirely by a state permit and a private settlement — no CWA discharge permit ever bounded the withdrawal — showing how data-center water limits in the Southeast rest on state allocation law and advocacy settlements rather than federal enforcement.

Details — violation, outcome, statute applicability & sources
Violation

Google sought to roughly triple its permitted withdrawal to about 1.5 million gallons per day (~547 million gallons/year) from the Middendorf Aquifer to cool an expanded server farm, even as DHEC was separately asking the town of Mount Pleasant to cut its aquifer use. After public opposition, the December 16, 2019 settlement restricts Google to using groundwater only 'as a last resort when all other water supplies are exhausted,' requires it to develop alternative supplies (surface water from Charleston Water System), and mandates public quarterly use reporting instead of the annual reporting otherwise required by law.

Outcome

Settlement in force since December 2019; Google was steered toward surface water and capped its routine groundwater draw. Subsequent withdrawal upticks have drawn renewed local scrutiny over aquifer depletion.

How statutes could apply
Statute applicability: SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
Pure consumption — the CWA regulates discharges, not withdrawals, and §316(b) intake rules only reach facilities pulling >2 MGD directly from surface waters. A CWA hook would require a discharge: blowdown to surface water (§402) or to the sewer (§307 pretreatment).
Historic examples in this record: Entergy v Riverkeeper (2009) · Swift Beef Grand Island (2011)
No CWA action — a state groundwater-withdrawal permit under SC DHEC plus a negotiated settlement with SELC/CCL governing the ~83-million-year-old Middendorf Aquifer. Groundwater allocation is state law, not federal CWA discharge regulation.
Sources
Groundwater Withdrawal: Google and Southern Environmental Law Center Enter into Agreement Addressing South Carolina Aquifer (legal analysis) · Google agrees to limit groundwater use from S.C. aquifer (advocacy/legal) · Google Groundwater Permit (project page) (advocacy)

Part 4 — Data-Center Sites with Reported Water Issues or Pushback (18)

18 named sites with documented water problems or community pushback — supply strain, dried wells, discharge fights, secrecy, moratoriums. Each card maps the fact pattern to the statutory readings from Part 1 that could reach it, citing the historical cases that show each reading in use. Readings overlap by design.

Google — The Dalles, ORDC site
The Dalles, Wasco County, OregonCWASDWAResolved — data disclosed; the case is now the template other communities cite when operators claim water use is confidential.
Water issue

Google's water use nearly tripled in five years to over a quarter of everything the city supplies (later reporting puts it near 40%). When The Oregonian requested the numbers in 2021, the city — with Google paying its legal fees — sued the newspaper to keep them secret as a 'trade secret.'

Pushback & response

The district attorney sided with the newspaper; the city settled in late 2022 and released ten years of data, and Google said it would stop claiming site-level water figures as trade secrets nationwide — the landmark transparency precedent for the industry.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
A single customer taking a quarter-plus of a small city's supply concentrates public-water-system capacity and compliance risk on everyone else's taps — the SDWA regime that receivership-era enforcement polices.
Historical cases: US v Alisal Water Corp (2005)
CWA — Pretreatment — industrial users of sewers
Cooling water Google evaporates or returns goes through the municipal system; blowdown returned to the sewer sits under the POTW's industrial-user pretreatment authority.
Historical cases: PortOfMorrow DEQ OR (2024)
Details — tracked cases, sources

xAI Colossus — Memphis, TNDC site
South Memphis, Tennessee (former Electrolux site)CWASDWAOperating on aquifer-sourced water; recycling plant paused with a promised Q1 2027 restart.
Water issue

Colossus draws roughly 3 MGD of cooling water while the Memphis Sand aquifer — the region's sole drinking-water source — is already being pumped faster than it recharges. xAI promised an $80M recycling plant treating up to ~13 MGD of municipal wastewater to offset withdrawals, then put it on indefinite pause in April 2026 to prioritize Colossus 2.

Pushback & response

Protect Our Aquifer called the pause 'alarming'; the city council pressed for public meetings on water impacts, and Mayor Young says xAI committed to resume construction no later than Q1 2027.

Which statutory readings could apply
CWA — NPDES permit for direct discharge
The promised recycling plant would hold its own NPDES permit — a clean, trackable point source whose DMRs become public the day it operates.
Historical cases: PortOfMorrow DEQ OR (2024)
CWA — Pretreatment — industrial users of sewers
Until then, cooling blowdown to the Memphis sewer sits under MLGW/City industrial-pretreatment authority, and Colossus-scale loading is exactly the pass-through risk the POTW's permit carries.
Historical cases: Swift Beef Grand Island (2011) · PortOfMorrow DEQ OR (2024)
SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
The Memphis Sand is a delineated sole drinking-water source; source-water assessments give residents a government-authored basis to contest withdrawals and siting over recharge areas.
Historical cases: Google Berkeley SC Middendorf aquifer (2019)

Meta — Newton County / Mansfield, GADC site
Social Circle / Mansfield area, Newton County, GeorgiaCWASDWAOngoing dispute; no formal enforcement yet.
Water issue

After Meta broke ground on its $750M campus, nearby well-dependent households — the closest about 1,000 feet away — reported taps running dry, sediment, and pressure loss. The facility uses ~500,000 gal/day (~10% of county water use), and Mansfield's mayor cited 33% water-rate increases over two years. Meta's commissioned study called its impact 'unlikely.'

Pushback & response

The July 2025 New York Times investigation made this the national reference case for data-center groundwater impacts; EPA has pledged investigation of the well complaints in adjoining Morgan County.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Emergency powers — imminent endangerment of drinking water
If construction dewatering or site releases are shown to have contaminated or dried domestic wells, EPA's emergency powers reach it on a 'contributed to' standard without waiting for a permit violation.
Historical cases: Trinity American v EPA SDWA (1431-1998) · DuPont WashingtonWorks PFOA (2005-2009)
SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
Wellhead-protection delineations around the affected domestic and municipal wells are the evidentiary frame for the county's zoning and permitting response.
Historical cases: Google Berkeley SC Middendorf aquifer (2019)
CWA — Construction stormwater (CGP)
The reported sediment in neighbors' water during mass grading is the classic construction-general-permit fact pattern.
Historical cases: Google Stillwater OK stormwater (2025) · Microsoft Boydton VA (2023)

Project Blue — Tucson / Pima County, AZDC site
Tucson / Pima County, ArizonaSDWACity rejection stands; redesigned county-land project proceeding without city water.
Water issue

The first two sites would have needed ~2,000 acre-feet of water per year — making the campus Tucson Water's largest customer — negotiated under NDAs that kept the end user secret. The city council rejected annexation 7-0 on August 6, 2025 after intense community pressure.

Pushback & response

AWS pulled out in late 2025; Beale is proceeding on county land outside city limits with a redesigned air-cooled plan while Tucson cut off water service and sought payback — the clearest municipal-veto precedent over data-center water demand.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
The fight was over a public system's obligation to its existing users — the demand-concentration risk the SDWA regime polices.

SDWA — Sole-source aquifer project review
Tucson sits over the EPA-designated Upper Santa Cruz / Avra Basin sole-source aquifer: any federally assisted piece of the project (loans, grants, federal infrastructure money) triggers EPA contamination review with funding-denial teeth.

Meta (Mesa) & Microsoft (Goodyear) — Phoenix metro, AZDC site
Mesa and Goodyear, Maricopa County, ArizonaSDWABoth operating and expanding under negotiated water commitments.
Water issue

Roughly 91 data centers operate in metro Phoenix amid a potential 77% cut to Arizona's Colorado River allocation. Meta's Mesa water agreement allows up to ~4 MGD at full build (with a pledge not to use Colorado River water and to transfer storage credits once demand exceeds 500,000 gal/day); Microsoft's Goodyear campus draws substantial potable water daily.

Pushback & response

Sustained criticism that the build-out continues through shortage; the operators' answer is mitigation — water-rights offsets, ~64.9M gal/yr of N-Drip restoration credits, and Microsoft's pledge of zero-water closed-loop designs for new Arizona builds.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
Municipal systems absorbing multi-MGD contracts during a Colorado River shortage carry the compliance and capacity risk for all other users.

PW Digital Gateway — Prince William County, VADC site
Pageland Lane corridor, Prince William County, Virginia — Occoquan Reservoir headwatersCWASDWAHalted — rezonings voided; project dead absent a restart.
Water issue

A 2,100+ acre, up-to-23M sq ft, 37-building rezoning was approved in December 2023 over objections from Fairfax Water and downstream supervisors: the corridor drains to the Occoquan Reservoir, which supplies roughly 40% of Northern Virginia's drinking water (800,000+ people), and the county declined to wait for a water-quality study.

Pushback & response

Residents and the American Battlefield Trust sued; on March 31, 2026 the Virginia Court of Appeals unanimously voided the rezonings, and the county — having spent $1.72M defending them — withdrew from further appeals. The largest data-center project yet stopped primarily on process, with drinking-water protection at the center of the record.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Source-water and wellhead protection
The Occoquan is a delineated source-water area for two utilities; the assessments and the reservoir's protective overlay were the government-authored backbone of the objections.
Historical cases: Google Berkeley SC Middendorf aquifer (2019)
CWA — Construction stormwater (CGP)
Grading thousands of acres in a reservoir's headwaters is the largest construction-stormwater exposure in the record — sediment control under the CGP would have been the binding operational constraint.
Historical cases: Microsoft Boydton VA (2023) · Energix VA (2024)
CWA — Dredge-and-fill of wetlands and streams
Campus build-out across the corridor's headwater streams would require Corps §404 authorization with §401 state certification.
Historical cases: Google ProjectRaspberry VA (2026) · SDC ATLA DouglasCounty GA 404 401 (2025)
CWA — Water-quality standards and antidegradation review
The Occoquan carries nutrient/sediment water-quality constraints; new loads in its watershed inherit those limits through any permit issued.
Historical cases: AWS LakeAnnaVA VPDES cooling discharge (2026)

AWS Lake Anna Technology Campus — Louisa County, VADC site
Louisa County, Virginia — Sedges Creek, Lake Anna tributaryCWATSCARHADEQ finalizing its decision after the June hearing; permit not yet issued as of early July 2026.
Water issue

A draft VPDES individual permit would let AWS discharge up to 280,000 gal/day of treated cooling water into Sedges Creek — the first direct-to-surface-water hyperscaler cooling-discharge permit in Virginia. Residents packed a March 2026 hearing, and a June 9, 2026 DEQ meeting turned contentious over chemical additives and the draft's lack of required PFAS testing (DEQ kept only a reopener condition).

Pushback & response

100+ residents at hearings demanding additive disclosure, PFAS monitoring, and testing transparency before the lake's recreation and drinking-water uses absorb a new industrial outfall.

Which statutory readings could apply
CWA — NPDES permit for direct discharge
This IS the NPDES reading in action — once issued, every exceedance of the permit's numeric limits is ECHO-visible and enforceable, including by citizen suit.
Historical cases: Smithfield Pagan River (1997) · Hanover Foods (2025)
CWA — Thermal discharge and cooling-water intake
A cooling-water discharge to a recreation lake puts §316(a) thermal review squarely into the permit record.
Historical cases: Brayton Point Dominion (2003-2007) · Greenidge SenecaLake NY (2025)
CWA — Water-quality standards and antidegradation review
Commenters invoked antidegradation — a new outfall to high-quality waters must survive an alternatives analysis (closed-loop or dry cooling).
RHA — Structures and obstructions in navigable waters
Any intake or outfall structure placed in navigable waters needs Corps §10 authorization on top of the VPDES permit.
Historical cases: US v Republic Steel RHA (1960)
TSCA — One-time PFAS manufacture/import reporting
The PFAS fight over the draft permit will eventually be armed by §8(a)(7) reporting data tracing fluorochemicals in cooling additives to their suppliers.
Historical cases: TSCA PFAS Reporting Rule (2023) · Chemours Washington Works PFAS (2023)

Amazon/AWS — Boardman & Umatilla, OR (Port of Morrow)DC site
Morrow and Umatilla counties, Oregon — Lower Umatilla BasinCWASDWARCRASettlement announced March 31, 2026; underlying reuse-and-land-application system still operating.
Water issue

Nitrate-laden Port of Morrow wastewater is used for AWS server cooling; evaporation concentrates the nitrates before the water is land-applied to farm fields above an aquifer where the state has found 634+ domestic wells with unsafe nitrate levels. Residents sued 17 defendants in February 2024.

Pushback & response

Amazon agreed in March 2026 to pay $20.5M (no admission of liability) — the first eight-figure direct-hyperscaler water settlement. Advocates continue pressing Amazon to treat the wastewater and slow expansion.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Emergency powers — imminent endangerment of drinking water
Contaminated domestic wells from industrial land application is the exact fact pattern on which EPA's §1431 order was upheld in Trinity American — the federal emergency hook remains available while the basin's wells stay unsafe.
Historical cases: Trinity American v EPA SDWA (1431-1998) · Flint MI SDWA (1431-2016)
RCRA — Imminent-and-substantial-endangerment actions (solid waste → groundwater)
Nitrate-bearing process wastewater applied to land that endangers groundwater supports a RCRA imminent-endangerment citizen suit — no CWA jurisdictional water needed.
Historical cases: Interfaith v Honeywell RCRA (2003-2005)
CWA — ‘Functional equivalent’ discharge through groundwater
Where land-applied water migrates through groundwater to the Umatilla or Columbia, Maui's functional-equivalent test can require an NPDES permit for the application itself.
Historical cases: County of Maui v Hawaii Wildlife Fund (2020)
CWA — Pretreatment — industrial users of sewers
The cooling loop's loading on the Port of Morrow's permit is what drew the port its own $727K penalty — the receiving-plant exposure this tracker was built to watch.
Historical cases: PortOfMorrow DEQ OR (2024)
Details — tracked cases, sources

Missouri municipal backlash — Peculiar & St. Charles, MODC site
Peculiar (Cass County) and St. Charles, MissouriSDWAPeculiar project dead; St. Charles moratorium era continuing with no state framework.
Water issue

Peculiar residents defeated a proposed data center in 2024 over water-rate, traffic, noise, and light concerns — a fight so acrimonious the city administrator and mayor resigned. St. Charles followed in 2025 as one of the first U.S. cities to pass a temporary data-center moratorium, driven partly by water-supply concerns; April 2026 municipal elections statewide showed data centers becoming an electoral liability.

Pushback & response

With the legislature passing no data-center regulation in 2026, Missouri's fights are being decided city by city — moratoriums, rejections, and council turnover.

Which statutory readings could apply
SDWA — Public water system regulation and enforcement
Small municipal systems are the regulatory pressure point when the state provides no siting framework — their SDWA obligations to existing users are the concrete stake behind the rate and supply objections.

Data Center Water News

Curated headlines on data center water regulation, enforcement, research, and solutions — linked to this tracker's datasets where applicable. Newest first.

34 items

ITIF Report: Data Center Water Problem Is 'Soluble' But Needs Coordinated State Policy
Information Technology and Innovation Foundation (ITIF) · 2026-07
An ITIF report by Robin Gaster argues data centers directly consumed ~17.4 billion gallons for cooling in 2023 versus ~211 billion gallons indirectly through electricity generation (about 12x more), and that water stress is regional (Pacific Northwest power generation uses 2.1 gal/kWh vs. 0.13 gal/kWh in solar-heavy California) rather than a uniform national problem. It recommends mandatory standardized water-use disclosure, watershed-based performance standards instead of technology mandates, integrated water-energy agency review, and a federal role limited to metrics/R&D/procurement rather than preempting state water law.
ResearchPolicy
Nearly One-Third of Indiana Counties Now Restrict Data Centers
WFYI Public Media · 2026-07
An Indiana University Environmental Resilience Institute tally found 11 Indiana counties have passed data-center zoning ordinances, at least 17 have enacted temporary moratoriums, and two (Marshall and Cass counties) have full bans — making Indiana one of the most restricted states for new data-center siting. Cited drivers include water, electricity, land, noise, and air-quality concerns; the Indianapolis Metropolitan Development Commission separately voted 5-3 to recommend new zoning rules, and Meta's $10 billion Lebanon, IN project and a contested Martinsville-Brightwood project were cited as flashpoints.
PolicyRegulation
Spartanburg County, SC Freezes Data-Center Applications for One Year Amid Water/Air Permit Fight
Post and Courier / Fox Carolina · 2026-06
Spartanburg County Council unanimously passed first reading of a 12-month data-center application moratorium on June 22, 2026, invoking the 'pending ordinance doctrine' to immediately freeze new applications — including a proposed Lighthouse Data Centers/Wharton Digital project at 300 Jones Road. The vote came days before SC DES held a June 25 public hearing on air and water discharge permits and increased power demand for the separate, already-under-construction Valara Holdings data center at the former Kohler plant site, which is exempt from the new moratorium. Part of a broader wave: several other SC counties have enacted or begun similar pauses in the absence of statewide rules.
RegulationPolicy
Amazon Discloses First-Ever Global Data Center Water Use Figure: 2.5 Billion Gallons in 2025
Latitude Media · 2026-06
Amazon disclosed for the first time that its global data centers used 2.5 billion gallons of water in 2025, reporting a water usage effectiveness of 0.03 gallons/kWh — a 52% efficiency improvement since 2021 — and claiming it returned roughly two-thirds of that water to communities through infrastructure investment. AWS says its total water withdrawal decreased from 2024 to 2025 even as data center capacity expanded, a reversal of the industry-wide trend; the disclosure puts Amazon alongside Google, Meta, and Microsoft in publishing aggregate water figures (though still only company-wide totals, not facility-level).
Data & ReportsPolicy
→ Substantiates the existing wue-reporting solution entry in the Solutions tab
New York's Passed Data Center Bill Is a One-Year DEC Permit Moratorium, Not Just a SEQRA Trigger
Greenberg Traurig · 2026-06
Legal analysis of the Responsible Data Center Development Act (A11560/S10642), passed by the legislature in early June and awaiting Gov. Hochul's signature, clarifies the bill imposes a one-year moratorium on new DEC permits/approvals for data centers of 20+ MW (exempting projects that have already started construction) — a stronger mechanism than a SEQRA environmental-review trigger alone. It requires water- and wastewater-impact disclosure at mandatory public hearings, a statewide environmental review of water use/discharge/thermal impacts, utility cost-recovery rules so data centers (not ratepayers) bear water-infrastructure upgrade costs, and host-community benefit programs addressing water impacts and cooling technology.
RegulationPolicy
→ Clarifies the mechanism for NY S10642/A11560 in the Legislation tab is a DEC permit moratorium, not only a SEQRA review trigger
Missed Water Meter Let Georgia QTS Data Center Rack Up $147,000 Unbilled Bill
The Citizen (Fayette County, GA) · 2026-05
A water meter serving Quality Technology Services' (QTS, owned by Blackstone) 615-acre 'Project Excalibur' data center campus in Fayetteville, Fayette County, GA was missed during the county's transition to smart advanced metering infrastructure, leaving roughly 13-15 million gallons of usage over six months uncaptured until residents in a nearby subdivision reported falling water pressure. Fayette County billed QTS about $147,000 retroactively (no fine was imposed), and county officials characterized it as an administrative oversight during the meter-system changeover rather than unauthorized water use.

Florida's Data Center Ratepayer and Reclaimed-Water Law Takes Effect
Data Center Dynamics / Fla. Governor's Office · 2026-07
FL SB 484 — signed by Gov. DeSantis in May, bars utilities from passing data-center electricity costs onto residential ratepayers, requires 50+ MW users to pay full cost of service, and mandates reclaimed water for cooling at facilities using 100,000+ gallons/day where feasible — took effect July 1, 2026. Local governments keep zoning/denial authority, and the law tightens aquifer withdrawal permitting.

Data Center Developer Who Vowed to Skip the Colorado River Now Sues For It
KPBS / Associated Press · 2026-06
Imperial Valley Computer Manufacturing, which spent months publicly pledging its planned Imperial County AI complex would not touch a drop of Colorado River water, is now suing the Imperial Irrigation District for 260 million gallons/year after IID rejected its water application and cities' negotiations broke down. The developer argues fallowing 160 leased acres of farmland offsets the ~750,000 gal/day cooling demand, and challenges IID's tiered pricing (industrial rates up to ~$1,100/acre-foot vs. ~$20/acre-foot for agricultural use) as an unlawful barrier.

Proposed Data Center Would Draw From Lake Supplying Most of Fort Worth's Drinking Water
Fort Worth Report · 2026-07
Kansas-based Diode Ventures has proposed a Henderson County data center that could draw up to 5 million gallons/day from Cedar Creek Lake, the reservoir supplying most of Fort Worth's drinking water, as area residents are asked to conserve amid drought-driven moderate water shortages. The West Cedar Creek Municipal Utility District had already advanced a temporary suspension of new 'high-intensity water service commitments' through April 1, 2027; the project's site review remains in early stages.

Jefferson County, PA Borough Passes 180-Day Data-Center Moratorium Over Water Supply
Pennsylvania Capital-Star · 2026-06
Brookville Borough Council unanimously passed a 180-day moratorium on new data-center development and barred projects from drawing on borough water, after two prospective developments in nearby Pine Creek and Rose townships surfaced that could together use roughly 2.4 million gallons/day. The council says it isn't opposed to data centers in principle but wants rules in place — a December deadline — before any project proceeds. Adds to the wave of small-jurisdiction moratoriums enacted ahead of any state-level Pennsylvania framework.
RegulationPolicy
Missouri Lawmakers Pass No Data-Center Water/Energy Rules; Local Moratoriums Keep Spreading
KCUR · 2026-07
Missouri's 2026 session ended without advancing HB 3362 (the 'AI Infrastructure Grid Integrity and Water Resources Protection Act'), which would have required a DNR permit for any facility using more than 2 million gallons/day of fresh water and included a drought/grid-emergency kill switch. With no state rule in place, communities are increasingly acting on their own — in Festus, four city council members lost their seats after supporting a $6B data center, and Independence councilmembers were voted out after backing a data-center tax break.
Policy
Most Texas Data Centers Ignore State Water-Use Survey
Texas Tribune / KUT · 2026-06
Fewer than a third of Texas's 341 data centers responded to a Public Utility Commission survey on water use — only 28 companies covering 92 facilities answered — prompting lawmakers to call for mandatory disclosure. The non-response rate is the clearest measurement yet of the industry's voluntary-transparency gap in the largest DC growth market.

Nvidia Claims AI's Water Challenge Is 'Largely Solved' With Warm-Liquid Cooling
Axios / Fortune · 2026-06
At London Climate Week, Nvidia said its newest AI systems can be cooled entirely with liquid warm enough to skip evaporative chilling, 'eliminating pretty much all water usage' in new designs — a supply-side answer to the industry's water problem. Independent verification does not yet exist, and the claim covers new builds, not the installed base.

Imperial County walked back its approval of what would be California's largest data center (1M+ sq ft), declaring a 45-day moratorium and forming a zoning commission; the developer sued for a temporary restraining order, arguing no true emergency exists. A live test of whether emergency land-use powers can pause an already-approved campus.

Confirms the pattern that Texas counties lack a legal vehicle to slow data-center siting.

More Than Two Dozen North Carolina Communities Restrict Data Centers
WRAL · 2026-06
Over 25 NC communities have adopted moratoriums or zoning restrictions on data centers amid drought-era concerns over water, power, and noise — the densest cluster of local pauses in the country, capped by Charlotte's 150-day citywide moratorium. National context: US data-center water demand could reach 73B gal/yr by 2028, up from ~17B in 2023.

Data-Center Moratorium Bills Introduced in 11 States in 2026
Good Jobs First · 2026-06
State-level moratorium bills have been introduced in 11 states in 2026; most face resistance, while dozens of municipalities move ahead with local construction pauses instead. The state/local split — statewide bills stall, city moratoriums pass — is becoming the defining pattern of the backlash's second year.

UN University: AI Data Centers' 2030 Water Footprint Equals Basic Needs of 1.3B People
United Nations University · 2026-06
UN scientists project that by 2030 global AI data centers' water footprint will equal the basic annual domestic water needs of all 1.3 billion people in Sub-Saharan Africa, alongside electricity use tripling the combined consumption of Pakistan, Bangladesh, and Nigeria — the first UN-system quantification of AI's aggregate water draw.

The Responsible Data Center Development Act (S10642 / A11560) passed the Senate 44-16 and Assembly 102-39, mandating SEQRA environmental review for any facility exceeding 50 MW. If signed, it would be the broadest state-level environmental review trigger for data centers in the US. Gov. Hochul has not publicly committed as of June 2026.

Google filed individual §404 permit applications for Project Raspberry (VA), Project Loch (VA), and a Port of Little Rock (AR) site. The Arkansas application involves 16.8 acres of wetland fill — the largest §404 wetland footprint in any active data center permit. Utah Governor's Office · 2026-05
Gov. Spencer Cox signed EO 2026-03 directing state agencies to require water-use impact review before approving large data center projects. The EO also instructs the Department of Natural Resources to evaluate cost-attribution models so developers bear marginal supply expansion costs. It complements Utah HB 76 (enacted March 2026), which mandates projected and actual water disclosure at a 10,000 sq ft threshold.

Virginia Governor's Office · 2026-04
Gov. Spanberger signed HB 496 (companion SB 553) on April 13, 2026, amending Va. Code § 62.1-44.38 to require water utilities to report monthly aggregate water volumes delivered to data centers. The law is effective July 1, 2026; first published data is expected late 2026. It is the first enacted state law mandating utility-level DC water reporting in the US.

Amazon Pays $20.5M in First Eight-Figure Hyperscaler Water Settlement
Oregon Department of Justice / Oregon DEQ · 2026-03
Amazon and its WWTP operator settled with Oregon DEQ and DOJ over nitrogen discharge at the Boardman industrial park — the first direct hyperscaler water-penalty case to reach eight figures. Data center cooling-tower blowdown was among the industrial loads routed to the facility. The settlement funds nutrient trading credits in the Columbia River basin.

Idaho Becomes First State to Mandate Closed-Loop (Non-Consumptive) Cooling for Data Centers
Idaho Legislature · 2026-03
Idaho H895 requires new data center facilities to use closed-loop or non-consumptive cooling systems as a condition of permitting. This eliminates the largest pathway of consumptive water loss — evaporation from open cooling towers. The law is designed to protect groundwater in the Snake River Plain aquifer system.

South Dakota Enacts 'Data Center Bill of Rights' with Water-Supply Compatibility Rule
South Dakota Legislature · 2026-03
SB 135 requires data centers ≥10 MW to demonstrate water-supply compatibility before construction approval, and mandates cost-causation billing so developers bear the marginal cost of supply-infrastructure expansions they trigger — not residential ratepayers. Part of a broader 'bill of rights' package addressing grid and water load-shifting.

Utah Enacts Water Transparency Amendments Lowering Disclosure Threshold to 10,000 sq ft
Utah Legislature · 2026-03
Utah HB 76 mandates that data centers ≥10,000 sq ft publicly report projected and actual water consumption annually. The 10,000 sq ft threshold was set to align with existing utility metering capabilities. It is a successor to earlier bills that stalled over data-collection infrastructure concerns.

Newsom vetoed in October 2024 citing data-collection concerns. Companion SB 887 would add CEQA review requirements. Both bills are expected to see floor votes in summer 2026.

Central Ohio Study Projects Data Center Water Demand Growing from 10 to 90 MGD by 2050
Central Ohio Regional Water Authority / Ohio EPA · 2025-03
A regional water demand study published March 2025 projects industrial water demand — dominated by planned data center expansions — growing from ~10 MGD today to 40 MGD by 2030 and 90 MGD by 2050. Intel's New Albany campus alone accounts for ~6 MGD. The study is being used to plan $2B+ in regional water infrastructure.

Ohio EPA · 2025-01
Comment period closed January 2025 on Ohio EPA OHD000001 — the first proposed general permit requiring data centers to file Discharge Monitoring Reports (DMRs) directly for cooling-tower blowdown. Currently, most DC cooling water routes through municipal WWTPs with no facility-level accounting. Final permit is still pending.

UC Riverside 'Making AI Less Thirsty' — First Major Quantification of AI Water Footprint
University of California, Riverside (Shaolei Ren et al.) · 2023-06
The landmark study estimated 0.5 L per 10–50 ChatGPT queries (direct cooling only) and up to 500 mL per query when including thermoelectric cooling for electricity generation. It catalyzed policy and media attention on data center water use and established WUE (Water Usage Effectiveness) as a public-facing metric. The viral 'one 500mL bottle per prompt' figure in later coverage was a 50–250× overstatement of this research.
